My son was just a little guy then, maybe seven or eight. We bought this little red plastic shovel from the hardware store downtown. Mac's Hardware, it was called. Old Mac, he always had a cigar hanging out of his mouth, even when he was ringing you up at the register. You don't see that anymore, people smoking right inside the stores. \n\nNow that whole building is one of those fancy coffee shops. They charge you four or five bucks just for a regular black coffee! And they hand it to you in a paper cup with a little cardboard sleeve so you don't burn your fingers. Back when my wife and I used to go to the diner, a cup of joe was a quarter, and they'd pour it in a real ceramic mug. Thick ones, you know? White with a little green stripe around the rim. Kept the heat in nicely. They had the best cherry pie there, too... \n\nAnyway, what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. No, I don't really have a lot of anxiety or anything like that. I don't have much going on to get worked up over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time lately, even when you're just sitting at home?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I'm just sitting there in the chair, like I said. Mostly just watching the street out the front window. \n\nThat window needs a good washing, actually. I used to do it every spring with a bucket of water and a splash of white vinegar. You crumple up an old newspaper to wipe it down, leaves absolutely no streaks. My mother taught me that when I was just a boy. She swore by it. \n\nIt's hard to get a decent newspaper to use for it these days, though. They stopped printing the local Tribune on Tuesdays and Thursdays, if you can believe that. And the kid who delivers the Sunday edition, he doesn't even come up the driveway. He just tosses it from his bicycle and half the time it ends up right in the middle of the rhododendron bushes by the porch. \n\nThose bushes are getting completely overgrown, now that I think about it. I should probably trim them back, but the clippers are all the way in the back of the garage behind the old push mower. It's a reel mower, you know? No motor, no gas, just human power and heavy iron blades. Gives the grass a nice clean cut, not like these loud gas guzzlers everybody uses now that just tear the grass up and make a racket on a Sunday morning. I used to love the smell of cut grass. Reminds me of playing baseball down at the sandlot...\n\nBut, uh... what was it you just asked me? Sad? No, no, I don't think I'm sad. Just quiet around the house, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in doing things you used to enjoy, like watching baseball or going to church?\n\nPatient: Interest? Well, I don't know about that. I haven't really been doing much of anything lately, to be honest. Just sitting in that chair. \n\nI used to go to church every Sunday, though. First Presbyterian, right over on Elm Street. The brick building with the big white steeple. They painted that steeple back in '98, I think. Hired a crew with one of those cherry picker trucks. The guy up in the bucket dropped his paintbrush and it landed right on Reverend Thomas's Buick. Left a big white splatter on the hood. The Reverend was pretty good-natured about it, though. He drove that Buick for another ten years with that paint spot right there for everyone to see. \n\nIt was a LeSabre, if I recall. Nice smooth ride. American made, back when that meant something. You could fit six people in that car comfortably. The seats were like big velour sofas. Not like these little plastic cars today, where you feel every bump in the road. My neighbor's got one of those little electric things. Plugs it into the side of his house like a toaster. Doesn't make a sound when he backs out of the driveway. Nearly ran over my foot last week when I was walking down to check the mail. \n\nThe mail carrier is a new guy. Young kid, always wearing those white earbuds. Doesn't even say hello. The old mailman, Gary, he knew everybody's name. Used to carry dog biscuits in his pocket for the neighborhood strays...\n\nBut what was it you were asking? Did I lose interest in things? I guess I just haven't been doing them. Not much point when you're just sitting around.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped caring about things you used to care about, like baseball or church, or do you still care but just haven't been doing them lately?\n\nPatient: Care? Oh, I don't know if it's about caring. I just haven't been going. \n\nI did use to care a lot about the baseball, though. Watched every game I could. The World Series back in '86, now that was something. I had this old Zenith television set, the kind with the wooden cabinet, you know? Weighed an absolute ton. Took two grown men just to move it across the living room. The picture tube finally went out on it in the middle of a thunderstorm. Lightning struck the transformer down the street—sounded like a cannon going off. Knocked the power out for three days. \n\nWe had to eat everything out of the freezer before it spoiled. My wife made this huge pot of chili with all the frozen ground beef. We ate chili for breakfast, lunch, and dinner. She used to put a little bit of cinnamon in her chili, just a pinch. Said it gave it a deeper flavor. Her grandmother taught her that. She came over from Italy on a boat when she was just a girl. Ellis Island and everything. They had to change their last name because the man at the desk couldn't spell it right...\n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Stopped caring? I don't know. I just sit in the chair mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer care about things like baseball or church, or is it more that you still care but can’t seem to make yourself do them anymore?\n\nPatient: Care? Well, I suppose I still care. It's not like I stopped liking them or anything. I just... I can't seem to get up and go do them. \n\nLike the church, you know. I still think about going. I even ironed my good trousers last Saturday night. Put a sharp crease right down the front. You have to use a damp cloth when you iron them, otherwise the fabric gets shiny. My wife used to have this heavy iron, a real old-fashioned one. You had to fill it with water from a little plastic cup, and it would hiss and spit steam everywhere. She burned her wrist on it once, right before my niece's wedding. We had to stop at the pharmacy for ointment on the way to the reception. \n\nThe reception was at this big hall downtown. They served chicken cordon bleu. It was a bit dry, if I'm being honest. But the band was good. They played all the old standards. Frank Sinatra, Dean Martin. I used to have a whole collection of their records on vinyl. Kept them in milk crates in the basement. The basement flooded back in 2005, ruined the whole lot of them. The water heater burst right in the middle of the night. Sounded like a waterfall down there. I had to call the plumber at three in the morning...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Making myself do things. Yeah, I just can't seem to get out of the chair these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or that you're failing the people who care about you, like your family or your church community?\n\nPatient: Failing people? Not worth much? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel like a failure or anything. I've always been a hard worker, earned my keep, you know? \n\nI worked stocking shelves and filling orders down at the grocery store for years. Best they had in the canned goods section. You have to rotate the stock, see—older cans in the front, newer in the back. A lot of the young kids they hire today just don't understand that. They just shove the new cans right on the front of the shelf. Like this one kid we had, Kevin. Nice enough boy, but he dropped a whole case of diced tomatoes once in the back storage room. What an absolute mess. Red juice everywhere, looked like a crime scene. We had to get the big industrial mop out. \n\nThat mop bucket had a squeaky wheel. Drove the floor manager, Mr. Henderson, completely up the wall. Squeak, squeak, squeak all the way down aisle four. Henderson was a stickler for noise. He used to wear these thick rubber-soled shoes so you couldn't hear him sneaking up behind you. Hush Puppies, I think they were called. My brother had a pair of those in brown suede. Wore them to a dance once and stepped in a deep mud puddle... ruined them completely. He tried to dry them by the radiator in the hallway and they shrunk up like curled leaves. My mother was so mad she made him pay for them out of his paper route money.\n\nAnyway... what did you ask me? About being worth something? No, I don't feel worthless. I just... haven't been doing much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I don't think so. I'm not restless or anything. I'm just sitting in my chair mostly. \n\nI mean, I'm not moving as fast as I used to, but I'm 74, you know? I used to be pretty quick on my feet. And behind the wheel, too. I had this 1968 Ford Torino. Now that car could move. Powder blue, with those vinyl seats that would absolutely burn the back of your legs in the summertime if you wore shorts. You had to roll the windows down for five minutes just to let the heat out before you could even sit. Hand-crank windows, none of this power button nonsense they put in cars today. The handle on the passenger side kept falling off, actually. I had to keep it in the glovebox. \n\nThe glovebox didn't latch right either, ever since my son slammed it too hard trying to kill a wasp that got in the car. Huge wasp, terrifying thing. We were on our way to the state fair when it happened. They had this giant pumpkin contest at the fair every year. I remember seeing one that weighed over eight hundred pounds. Took a whole forklift just to get it onto the scale. The farmer who grew it wore these bright striped overalls. Said his secret was using fish emulsion in the soil. Smelled awful, I imagine, but you can't argue with an eight-hundred-pound pumpkin...\n\nI'm sorry, what was the question? Something about sitting still? No, I'm not restless. Like I said, I just don't do much moving at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless inside, even if you're not showing it or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Down? No, I wouldn't use those words. I'm not sitting here crying or feeling sorry for myself or anything like that. I just... well, I mostly just sit in my chair. \n\nIt's a very good chair, to be fair. A genuine La-Z-Boy. I bought it at Sears back in... oh, must have been 1992. Back when they still had those massive catalogs, you remember those? You could order just about anything out of them. My mother actually ordered a whole sewing machine from one when I was a boy. A Singer. Heavy metal thing, painted black with gold trim, weighed an absolute ton. The mailman, Gary, nearly threw his back out carrying it up our front steps. Gary was a good fella. He wore those little blue uniform shorts even when it got cold in November. He had this beagle named Buster that he’d sometimes let ride in the mail truck. Buster had a terrible habit of chasing the neighborhood squirrels. One time, he chased a big fat gray one right up the oak tree in Mrs. Gable's yard, and the squirrel jumped onto her roof and got straight into her attic through a loose shingle. She had to call pest control, and they set up all these little wire traps baited with peanut butter... \n\nI'm sorry, my mind is wandering again. What did you ask me? About feeling down? No, I don't feel hopeless inside. Like I said, I just don't have the drive to get up and do much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The water heater burst right in the middle of the night. Sounded like a waterfall down there. I had to call the plumber at three in the morning...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Making myself do things. Yeah, I just can't seem to get out of the chair these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or that you're failing the people who care about you, like your family or your church community?\n\nPatient: Failing people? Not worth much? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel like a failure or anything. I've always been a hard worker, earned my keep, you know? \n\nI worked stocking shelves and filling orders down at the grocery store for years. Best they had in the canned goods section. You have to rotate the stock, see—older cans in the front, newer in the back. A lot of the young kids they hire today just don't understand that. They just shove the new cans right on the front of the shelf. Like this one kid we had, Kevin. Nice enough boy, but he dropped a whole case of diced tomatoes once in the back storage room. What an absolute mess. Red juice everywhere, looked like a crime scene. We had to get the big industrial mop out. \n\nThat mop bucket had a squeaky wheel. Drove the floor manager, Mr. Henderson, completely up the wall. Squeak, squeak, squeak all the way down aisle four. Henderson was a stickler for noise. He used to wear these thick rubber-soled shoes so you couldn't hear him sneaking up behind you. Hush Puppies, I think they were called. My brother had a pair of those in brown suede. Wore them to a dance once and stepped in a deep mud puddle... ruined them completely. He tried to dry them by the radiator in the hallway and they shrunk up like curled leaves. My mother was so mad she made him pay for them out of his paper route money.\n\nAnyway... what did you ask me? About being worth something? No, I don't feel worthless. I just... haven't been doing much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I don't think so. I'm not restless or anything. I'm just sitting in my chair mostly. \n\nI mean, I'm not moving as fast as I used to, but I'm 74, you know? I used to be pretty quick on my feet. And behind the wheel, too. I had this 1968 Ford Torino. Now that car could move. Powder blue, with those vinyl seats that would absolutely burn the back of your legs in the summertime if you wore shorts. You had to roll the windows down for five minutes just to let the heat out before you could even sit. Hand-crank windows, none of this power button nonsense they put in cars today. The handle on the passenger side kept falling off, actually. I had to keep it in the glovebox. \n\nThe glovebox didn't latch right either, ever since my son slammed it too hard trying to kill a wasp that got in the car. Huge wasp, terrifying thing. We were on our way to the state fair when it happened. They had this giant pumpkin contest at the fair every year. I remember seeing one that weighed over eight hundred pounds. Took a whole forklift just to get it onto the scale. The farmer who grew it wore these bright striped overalls. Said his secret was using fish emulsion in the soil. Smelled awful, I imagine, but you can't argue with an eight-hundred-pound pumpkin...\n\nI'm sorry, what was the question? Something about sitting still? No, I'm not restless. Like I said, I just don't do much moving at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless inside, even if you're not showing it or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Down? No, I wouldn't use those words. I'm not sitting here crying or feeling sorry for myself or anything like that. I just... well, I mostly just sit in my chair. \n\nIt's a very good chair, to be fair. A genuine La-Z-Boy. I bought it at Sears back in... oh, must have been 1992. Back when they still had those massive catalogs, you remember those? You could order just about anything out of them. My mother actually ordered a whole sewing machine from one when I was a boy. A Singer. Heavy metal thing, painted black with gold trim, weighed an absolute ton. The mailman, Gary, nearly threw his back out carrying it up our front steps. Gary was a good fella. He wore those little blue uniform shorts even when it got cold in November. He had this beagle named Buster that he’d sometimes let ride in the mail truck. Buster had a terrible habit of chasing the neighborhood squirrels. One time, he chased a big fat gray one right up the oak tree in Mrs. Gable's yard, and the squirrel jumped onto her roof and got straight into her attic through a loose shingle. She had to call pest control, and they set up all these little wire traps baited with peanut butter... \n\nI'm sorry, my mind is wandering again. What did you ask me? About feeling down? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy things just fine, I haven't lost interest. I just don't do them as much. 

Like baseball, I still like baseball. I used to listen to the games on this old Zenith transistor radio I had. Silver front, came in a little black leather case with a carrying strap. That strap eventually snapped, though. I tried to fix it with some duct tape, but the tape got all gummy in the summer heat. You know how duct tape gets when it's left in the sun? Leaves that awful sticky gray residue on absolutely everything it touches. I got it all over my good slacks once. Khaki pants I bought for my nephew's high school graduation. 

He went on to the state university to get a degree in... oh, what was it? Geology, I think. Rocks. He loved rocks. Used to carry a motorized rock tumbler right in the trunk of his car. Loudest thing you ever heard, just grinding and tumbling away back there while he drove. He drove a Honda Civic, one of those early ones from the seventies that looked like a little roller skate. It was bright yellow. You really don't see many yellow cars anymore, do you? Used to be you'd see yellow cars, mint green cars, two-tone cars with white roofs. Now everything on the highway is just silver or black or gray. It's very boring to look at when you're sitting out on the porch watching traffic go by. My neighbor, Mr. Wallace, he just bought a silver SUV. Barely fits in his driveway. He had to cut back his prize-winning hydrangea bushes just to open the passenger door...

I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Losing interest in things? No, no, I haven't lost interest. I still like what I like. I just... well, like I said, I mostly just sit in my chair these days.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess I came in because... I just feel like I’m completely falling apart. It really started ever since they changed my hours at the warehouse and put in that new inventory system. Everything is on these little digital scanners now. It's not like the old paper logs at all. I used to have this heavy-duty metal clipboard, the kind with the really strong spring at the top? You could drop it off a forklift and it wouldn't even dent. We had a guy on the loading dock, Tommy, who actually ran one over with a pallet jack once and... well, anyway, since all these changes at work, I'm just a mess. \n\nI've been missing my shifts. I'm even missing my church commitments, which isn't like me at all. I've been an usher there for twelve years. Pastor Dave even called my house the other day, and he never calls during the week unless somebody's in the hospital or something. And my wife says I'm just snapping at her and the rest of the family all the time now. It's gotten so bad that some mornings I just sit there on the edge of the bed in my work boots, just staring at the floor, wondering if I can even face going into that place anymore. They're good boots, too, Red Wings, got them a few years back because my arches were acting up on the concrete floors, but lately sitting there... they just feel like they weigh a hundred pounds each.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like your church or work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I'd say I'm feeling down, or... or losing interest, exactly. I don't think I've lost interest. I mean, I still care about the church. I know where everybody likes to sit, you know? Like the Hendersons, they always take the third row on the left side, right under that big stained glass window with the dove on it. \n\nThat window actually got a nasty crack in it during a bad hailstorm back in... oh, must have been 2018. We had ice the size of golf balls coming down. We had to get a special fellow all the way out from the city to fix the lead lining on it. Cost the congregation a pretty penny, but he did a beautiful job. He used this special putty, I think. You can't even tell it was ever broken now. \n\nBut... I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, about being down. I just... I don't really know about that. I just haven't been going. I just sit there on the bed and don't go. I guess I just don't have the drive for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about keeping up with the new system at work or making mistakes because of the changes?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if anxious is the right word, but... yeah, I guess I do worry about it. It's just that little scanner thing. It's got all these tiny buttons, and the screen is so small. My eyes aren't what they used to be, you know? I had to go get those reading glasses from the pharmacy, the ones on the spinning rack near the greeting cards. \n\nActually, the last time I was in there, they had reorganized the whole aisle. Put the cards where the shampoo used to be. It took me ten minutes just to find a birthday card for my grandson. He just turned twelve. Getting so tall, too. He plays baseball, pitches for his Little League team. He's got this windup that reminds me of... oh, who was that pitcher for the Mets back in the day? Tom Seaver! That's it. Tom Terrific. Man, he could throw a fastball. \n\nBut anyway, the scanner... yeah, I worry I'm going to press the wrong button and mess up the whole inventory count. It's just not like the old clipboard. You could see everything right there on the paper. Now it's all in the... the cloud, or whatever they call it. I just don't want to be the one who screws it all up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel especially nervous or panicky when you have to use the scanner or perform tasks that require you to keep up with the new system at work?\n\nPatient: Well, nervous... I mean, I guess my stomach does get a little tied up in knots when I have to pick the darn thing up. It's just so slick and black, like one of those smartphones my daughter is always staring at. She got one of those new ones for Christmas, the kind that folds right in half? I didn't even know they made phones that folded anymore. I remember back when everybody had those flip phones, Motorola I think they were called. I had a silver one. Kept it in a little leather holster on my belt. \n\nMy buddy Hank, he worked down at the hardware store before it closed, he had one too, but his had a little antenna you had to pull out with your teeth if your hands were full. Hank was a real character, always had a joke. He moved down to Florida a couple of years ago, near Tampa. Said he was tired of shoveling snow. I don't blame him, honestly. The snow blower I have is getting harder to start every year. The pull cord on it just feels like it takes everything I've got. \n\nBut, uh, what was it? Panicky? I don't... I don't really know about panicky. I just don't like using it. It just doesn't feel right in my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you’re not getting pleasure or satisfaction out of things that used to make you feel good, like seeing your grandson play baseball or going to church?\n\nPatient: Well, pleasure... I don't know if I'd say I'm not getting pleasure out of things. I just... I haven't really been doing much of anything lately to find out. Like the baseball games, I haven't been down to the field in a few weeks. The bleachers there, they're those old aluminum ones, you know? They get so hot in the sun, you could fry an egg on them. \n\nMy wife, God rest her soul, she used to bring this little square foam cushion to sit on. It had the local high school mascot printed on it—the fighting badgers, or maybe it was wolverines. No, badgers. She got it at a bake sale they were having to raise money for new band uniforms. The band always looked so sharp marching down Main Street during the Fourth of July parade. They had these big plumed hats. \n\nI used to love a good parade. The fire trucks would come by and toss candy to the kids. Mostly those little hard strawberry candies with the wrapper that looks like a strawberry, you know the ones? I don't even know where you buy those. I never see them at the grocery store, just at parades or in a bowl at the bank. The teller at my bank, Sheila, she always keeps a bowl of peppermints, though. \n\nBut, what were you asking? Satisfaction? I just... I don't really know. I just haven't been going to the games or the church, so I couldn't really tell you if I enjoy them right now. I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like watching your grandson play baseball or going to church, don’t bring you the same pleasure or satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess you asked me that already, didn't you? Sorry, my mind just wanders sometimes. But like I was saying, I just haven't been going to the games. Or church, for that matter. Pastor Miller, he gives a good sermon, but the pews in that place... they're solid oak, I think. Hard as a rock. \n\nThey did put in some new carpeting a few years back, though. A real deep burgundy color. It matches the hymn books. I used to sing in the choir, you know, back when I was in my thirties. I was a baritone. We had this one fellow, Arthur, who sang bass, and his voice was so deep it practically rattled the stained glass windows. He drove this old Ford pickup truck, one of those two-tone ones, blue and white. Kept it running like a top. \n\nAnyway, the pleasure part... I just don't know. Since I'm not doing those things, I can't really say if they bring me joy or not. I just sit in my recliner mostly. It's a La-Z-Boy. Got it on sale about five years ago...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling unusually tired or lacking energy most days, even when you're not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Hmm. I wouldn't really say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I don't feel drained or anything, I just... well, like I said, I'm mostly just sitting in that La-Z-Boy. I actually fall asleep in it more often than my actual bed these days. \n\nThe bed has one of those memory foam mattresses. Supposed to be good for your back, or that's what the fella on the TV commercial said. The delivery guys had a heck of a time getting it up the stairs, though. One of them, a young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, kept bumping the drywall. Left a big black scuff mark right next to the thermostat in the hallway. It's one of those old round Honeywell thermostats, you know the ones? With the gold dial. They don't make them like that anymore. Everything now is digital and beeps at you and connects to the internet. \n\nI don't know why a thermostat needs the internet. My buddy Hank, before he moved down to Florida, he got one of those smart ones and it would just turn the heat down on him in the middle of the night to save a few pennies. He'd wake up freezing, looking for an extra blanket. His wife used to knit these beautiful afghans, all sorts of colors... \n\nBut, uh... energy? Did you ask about energy? Yeah, no, I don't think my energy is gone. I just don't have much to use it on right now, since I'm just sitting around.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively or feeling intense fear about using the new inventory system at work, even when you’re not actually using it?\n\nPatient: Work? Oh, no, I... I don't work anymore. I'm retired. I used to be an order filler down at the warehouse, but I hung up my hat a few years back. The new inventory system... I don't know anything about that. When I was there, we used these big yellow clipboards and carbon paper. You had to press down real hard with your pen or the pink copy wouldn't be readable. \n\nI always used those Bic pens, the ones with the blue caps. You could buy a whole box of them for a couple of bucks at the drugstore. The drugstore on the corner, Miller's Pharmacy, they used to have a soda fountain in the back. Best cherry phosphates in town. Old man Miller would mix them himself. He wore these thick glasses, looked like the bottoms of Coca-Cola bottles. \n\nSpeaking of bottles, the recycling truck comes by on Tuesdays now instead of Thursdays. It really throws my whole week off. I put the bin out on Wednesday night and then I realize I'm a day late. The bin is one of those big blue ones with the wheels. The wheels always squeak when I roll it down the driveway. I tried putting some WD-40 on them, but it didn't seem to help much. WD-40 used to fix everything. My dad used it on the hinges of the screen door... \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking about? Fear? No, I don't think I'm afraid of anything at work, seeing as I don't go there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a lot of different things in your life, even when there isn't a clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Worried? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, what is there to worry about when you just sit in the house all day? \n\nI guess... well, I do think about the gutters sometimes. They get all clogged up with leaves this time of year. There's this massive oak tree out front. I planted it myself back in '82, I think it was. My son was just a little guy then, maybe seven or eight. We bought this little red plastic shovel from the hardware store downtown. Mac's Hardware, it was called. Old Mac, he always had a cigar hanging out of his mouth, even when he was ringing you up at the register. You don't see that anymore, people smoking right inside the stores. \n\nNow that whole building is one of those fancy coffee shops. They charge you four or five bucks just for a regular black coffee! And they hand it to you in a paper cup with a little cardboard sleeve so you don't burn your fingers. Back when my wife and I used to go to the diner, a cup of joe was a quarter, and they'd pour it in a real ceramic mug. Thick ones, you know? White with a little green stripe around the rim. Kept the heat in nicely. They had the best cherry pie there, too... \n\nAnyway, what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. No, I don't really have a lot of anxiety or anything like that. I don't have much going on to get worked up over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time lately, even when you're just sitting at home?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I'm just sitting there in the chair, like I said. Mostly just watching the street out the front window. \n\nThat window needs a good washing, actually. I used to do it every spring with a bucket of water and a splash of white vinegar. You crumple up an old newspaper to wipe it down, leaves absolutely no streaks. My mother taught me that when I was just a boy. She swore by it. \n\nIt's hard to get a decent newspaper to use for it these days, though. They stopped printing the local Tribune on Tuesdays and Thursdays, if you can believe that. And the kid who delivers the Sunday edition, he doesn't even come up the driveway. He just tosses it from his bicycle and half the time it ends up right in the middle of the rhododendron bushes by the porch. \n\nThose bushes are getting completely overgrown, now that I think about it. I should probably trim them back, but the clippers are all the way in the back of the garage behind the old push mower. It's a reel mower, you know? No motor, no gas, just human power and heavy iron blades. Gives the grass a nice clean cut, not like these loud gas guzzlers everybody uses now that just tear the grass up and make a racket on a Sunday morning. I used to love the smell of cut grass. Reminds me of playing baseball down at the sandlot...\n\nBut, uh... what was it you just asked me? Sad? No, no, I don't think I'm sad. Just quiet around the house, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in doing things you used to enjoy, like watching baseball or going to church?\n\nPatient: Interest? Well, I don't know about that. I haven't really been doing much of anything lately, to be honest. Just sitting in that chair. \n\nI used to go to church every Sunday, though. First Presbyterian, right over on Elm Street. The brick building with the big white steeple. They painted that steeple back in '98, I think. Hired a crew with one of those cherry picker trucks. The guy up in the bucket dropped his paintbrush and it landed right on Reverend Thomas's Buick. Left a big white splatter on the hood. The Reverend was pretty good-natured about it, though. He drove that Buick for another ten years with that paint spot right there for everyone to see. \n\nIt was a LeSabre, if I recall. Nice smooth ride. American made, back when that meant something. You could fit six people in that car comfortably. The seats were like big velour sofas. Not like these little plastic cars today, where you feel every bump in the road. My neighbor's got one of those little electric things. Plugs it into the side of his house like a toaster. Doesn't make a sound when he backs out of the driveway. Nearly ran over my foot last week when I was walking down to check the mail. \n\nThe mail carrier is a new guy. Young kid, always wearing those white earbuds. Doesn't even say hello. The old mailman, Gary, he knew everybody's name. Used to carry dog biscuits in his pocket for the neighborhood strays...\n\nBut what was it you were asking? Did I lose interest in things? I guess I just haven't been doing them. Not much point when you're just sitting around.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped caring about things you used to care about, like baseball or church, or do you still care but just haven't been doing them lately?\n\nPatient: Care? Oh, I don't know if it's about caring. I just haven't been going. \n\nI did use to care a lot about the baseball, though. Watched every game I could. The World Series back in '86, now that was something. I had this old Zenith television set, the kind with the wooden cabinet, you know? Weighed an absolute ton. Took two grown men just to move it across the living room. The picture tube finally went out on it in the middle of a thunderstorm. Lightning struck the transformer down the street—sounded like a cannon going off. Knocked the power out for three days. \n\nWe had to eat everything out of the freezer before it spoiled. My wife made this huge pot of chili with all the frozen ground beef. We ate chili for breakfast, lunch, and dinner. She used to put a little bit of cinnamon in her chili, just a pinch. Said it gave it a deeper flavor. Her grandmother taught her that. She came over from Italy on a boat when she was just a girl. Ellis Island and everything. They had to change their last name because the man at the desk couldn't spell it right...\n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Stopped caring? I don't know. I just sit in the chair mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer care about things like baseball or church, or is it more that you still care but can’t seem to make yourself do them anymore?\n\nPatient: Care? Well, I suppose I still care. It's not like I stopped liking them or anything. I just... I can't seem to get up and go do them. \n\nLike the church, you know. I still think about going. I even ironed my good trousers last Saturday night. Put a sharp crease right down the front. You have to use a damp cloth when you iron them, otherwise the fabric gets shiny. My wife used to have this heavy iron, a real old-fashioned one. You had to fill it with water from a little plastic cup, and it would hiss and spit steam everywhere. She burned her wrist on it once, right before my niece's wedding. We had to stop at the pharmacy for ointment on the way to the reception. \n\nThe reception was at this big hall downtown. They served chicken cordon bleu. It was a bit dry, if I'm being honest. But the band was good. They played all the old standards. Frank Sinatra, Dean Martin. I used to have a whole collection of their records on vinyl. Kept them in milk crates in the basement. The basement flooded back in 2005, ruined the whole lot of them. The water heater burst right in the middle of the night. Sounded like a waterfall down there. I had to call the plumber at three in the morning...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Making myself do things. Yeah, I just can't seem to get out of the chair these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or that you're failing the people who care about you, like your family or your church community?\n\nPatient: Failing people? Not worth much? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel like a failure or anything. I've always been a hard worker, earned my keep, you know? \n\nI worked stocking shelves and filling orders down at the grocery store for years. Best they had in the canned goods section. You have to rotate the stock, see—older cans in the front, newer in the back. A lot of the young kids they hire today just don't understand that. They just shove the new cans right on the front of the shelf. Like this one kid we had, Kevin. Nice enough boy, but he dropped a whole case of diced tomatoes once in the back storage room. What an absolute mess. Red juice everywhere, looked like a crime scene. We had to get the big industrial mop out. \n\nThat mop bucket had a squeaky wheel. Drove the floor manager, Mr. Henderson, completely up the wall. Squeak, squeak, squeak all the way down aisle four. Henderson was a stickler for noise. He used to wear these thick rubber-soled shoes so you couldn't hear him sneaking up behind you. Hush Puppies, I think they were called. My brother had a pair of those in brown suede. Wore them to a dance once and stepped in a deep mud puddle... ruined them completely. He tried to dry them by the radiator in the hallway and they shrunk up like curled leaves. My mother was so mad she made him pay for them out of his paper route money.\n\nAnyway... what did you ask me? About being worth something? No, I don't feel worthless. I just... haven't been doing much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I don't think so. I'm not restless or anything. I'm just sitting in my chair mostly. \n\nI mean, I'm not moving as fast as I used to, but I'm 74, you know? I used to be pretty quick on my feet. And behind the wheel, too. I had this 1968 Ford Torino. Now that car could move. Powder blue, with those vinyl seats that would absolutely burn the back of your legs in the summertime if you wore shorts. You had to roll the windows down for five minutes just to let the heat out before you could even sit. Hand-crank windows, none of this power button nonsense they put in cars today. The handle on the passenger side kept falling off, actually. I had to keep it in the glovebox. \n\nThe glovebox didn't latch right either, ever since my son slammed it too hard trying to kill a wasp that got in the car. Huge wasp, terrifying thing. We were on our way to the state fair when it happened. They had this giant pumpkin contest at the fair every year. I remember seeing one that weighed over eight hundred pounds. Took a whole forklift just to get it onto the scale. The farmer who grew it wore these bright striped overalls. Said his secret was using fish emulsion in the soil. Smelled awful, I imagine, but you can't argue with an eight-hundred-pound pumpkin...\n\nI'm sorry, what was the question? Something about sitting still? No, I'm not restless. Like I said, I just don't do much moving at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless inside, even if you're not showing it or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Down? No, I wouldn't use those words. I'm not sitting here crying or feeling sorry for myself or anything like that. I just... well, I mostly just sit in my chair. \n\nIt's a very good chair, to be fair. A genuine La-Z-Boy. I bought it at Sears back in... oh, must have been 1992. Back when they still had those massive catalogs, you remember those? You could order just about anything out of them. My mother actually ordered a whole sewing machine from one when I was a boy. A Singer. Heavy metal thing, painted black with gold trim, weighed an absolute ton. The mailman, Gary, nearly threw his back out carrying it up our front steps. Gary was a good fella. He wore those little blue uniform shorts even when it got cold in November. He had this beagle named Buster that he’d sometimes let ride in the mail truck. Buster had a terrible habit of chasing the neighborhood squirrels. One time, he chased a big fat gray one right up the oak tree in Mrs. Gable's yard, and the squirrel jumped onto her roof and got straight into her attic through a loose shingle. She had to call pest control, and they set up all these little wire traps baited with peanut butter... \n\nI'm sorry, my mind is wandering again. What did you ask me? About feeling down? No, I don't feel hopeless inside. Like I said, I just don't have the drive to get up and do much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even the things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy things just fine, I haven't lost interest. I just don't do them as much. \n\nLike baseball, I still like baseball. I used to listen to the games on this old Zenith transistor radio I had. Silver front, came in a little black leather case with a carrying strap. That strap eventually snapped, though. I tried to fix it with some duct tape, but the tape got all gummy in the summer heat. You know how duct tape gets when it's left in the sun? Leaves that awful sticky gray residue on absolutely everything it touches. I got it all over my good slacks once. Khaki pants I bought for my nephew's high school graduation. \n\nHe went on to the state university to get a degree in... oh, what was it? Geology, I think. Rocks. He loved rocks. Used to carry a motorized rock tumbler right in the trunk of his car. Loudest thing you ever heard, just grinding and tumbling away back there while he drove. He drove a Honda Civic, one of those early ones from the seventies that looked like a little roller skate. It was bright yellow. You really don't see many yellow cars anymore, do you? Used to be you'd see yellow cars, mint green cars, two-tone cars with white roofs. Now everything on the highway is just silver or black or gray. It's very boring to look at when you're sitting out on the porch watching traffic go by. My neighbor, Mr. Wallace, he just bought a silver SUV. Barely fits in his driveway. He had to cut back his prize-winning hydrangea bushes just to open the passenger door...\n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Losing interest in things? No, no, I haven't lost interest. I still like what I like. I just... well, like I said, I mostly just sit in my chair these days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. He'd wake up freezing, looking for an extra blanket. His wife used to knit these beautiful afghans, all sorts of colors... \n\nBut, uh... energy? Did you ask about energy? Yeah, no, I don't think my energy is gone. I just don't have much to use it on right now, since I'm just sitting around.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively or feeling intense fear about using the new inventory system at work, even when you’re not actually using it?\n\nPatient: Work? Oh, no, I... I don't work anymore. I'm retired. I used to be an order filler down at the warehouse, but I hung up my hat a few years back. The new inventory system... I don't know anything about that. When I was there, we used these big yellow clipboards and carbon paper. You had to press down real hard with your pen or the pink copy wouldn't be readable. \n\nI always used those Bic pens, the ones with the blue caps. You could buy a whole box of them for a couple of bucks at the drugstore. The drugstore on the corner, Miller's Pharmacy, they used to have a soda fountain in the back. Best cherry phosphates in town. Old man Miller would mix them himself. He wore these thick glasses, looked like the bottoms of Coca-Cola bottles. \n\nSpeaking of bottles, the recycling truck comes by on Tuesdays now instead of Thursdays. It really throws my whole week off. I put the bin out on Wednesday night and then I realize I'm a day late. The bin is one of those big blue ones with the wheels. The wheels always squeak when I roll it down the driveway. I tried putting some WD-40 on them, but it didn't seem to help much. WD-40 used to fix everything. My dad used it on the hinges of the screen door... \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking about? Fear? No, I don't think I'm afraid of anything at work, seeing as I don't go there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a lot of different things in your life, even when there isn't a clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Worried? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, what is there to worry about when you just sit in the house all day? \n\nI guess... well, I do think about the gutters sometimes. They get all clogged up with leaves this time of year. There's this massive oak tree out front. I planted it myself back in '82, I think it was. My son was just a little guy then, maybe seven or eight. We bought this little red plastic shovel from the hardware store downtown. Mac's Hardware, it was called. Old Mac, he always had a cigar hanging out of his mouth, even when he was ringing you up at the register. You don't see that anymore, people smoking right inside the stores. \n\nNow that whole building is one of those fancy coffee shops. They charge you four or five bucks just for a regular black coffee! And they hand it to you in a paper cup with a little cardboard sleeve so you don't burn your fingers. Back when my wife and I used to go to the diner, a cup of joe was a quarter, and they'd pour it in a real ceramic mug. Thick ones, you know? White with a little green stripe around the rim. Kept the heat in nicely. They had the best cherry pie there, too... \n\nAnyway, what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. No, I don't really have a lot of anxiety or anything like that. I don't have much going on to get worked up over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time lately, even when you're just sitting at home?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I'm just sitting there in the chair, like I said. Mostly just watching the street out the front window. \n\nThat window needs a good washing, actually. I used to do it every spring with a bucket of water and a splash of white vinegar. You crumple up an old newspaper to wipe it down, leaves absolutely no streaks. My mother taught me that when I was just a boy. She swore by it. \n\nIt's hard to get a decent newspaper to use for it these days, though. They stopped printing the local Tribune on Tuesdays and Thursdays, if you can believe that. And the kid who delivers the Sunday edition, he doesn't even come up the driveway. He just tosses it from his bicycle and half the time it ends up right in the middle of the rhododendron bushes by the porch. \n\nThose bushes are getting completely overgrown, now that I think about it. I should probably trim them back, but the clippers are all the way in the back of the garage behind the old push mower. It's a reel mower, you know? No motor, no gas, just human power and heavy iron blades. Gives the grass a nice clean cut, not like these loud gas guzzlers everybody uses now that just tear the grass up and make a racket on a Sunday morning. I used to love the smell of cut grass. Reminds me of playing baseball down at the sandlot...\n\nBut, uh... what was it you just asked me? Sad? No, no, I don't think I'm sad. Just quiet around the house, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in doing things you used to enjoy, like watching baseball or going to church?\n\nPatient: Interest? Well, I don't know about that. I haven't really been doing much of anything lately, to be honest. Just sitting in that chair. \n\nI used to go to church every Sunday, though. First Presbyterian, right over on Elm Street. The brick building with the big white steeple. They painted that steeple back in '98, I think. Hired a crew with one of those cherry picker trucks. The guy up in the bucket dropped his paintbrush and it landed right on Reverend Thomas's Buick. Left a big white splatter on the hood. The Reverend was pretty good-natured about it, though. He drove that Buick for another ten years with that paint spot right there for everyone to see. \n\nIt was a LeSabre, if I recall. Nice smooth ride. American made, back when that meant something. You could fit six people in that car comfortably. The seats were like big velour sofas. Not like these little plastic cars today, where you feel every bump in the road. My neighbor's got one of those little electric things. Plugs it into the side of his house like a toaster. Doesn't make a sound when he backs out of the driveway. Nearly ran over my foot last week when I was walking down to check the mail. \n\nThe mail carrier is a new guy. Young kid, always wearing those white earbuds. Doesn't even say hello. The old mailman, Gary, he knew everybody's name. Used to carry dog biscuits in his pocket for the neighborhood strays...\n\nBut what was it you were asking? Did I lose interest in things? I guess I just haven't been doing them. Not much point when you're just sitting around.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped caring about things you used to care about, like baseball or church, or do you still care but just haven't been doing them lately?\n\nPatient: Care? Oh, I don't know if it's about caring. I just haven't been going. \n\nI did use to care a lot about the baseball, though. Watched every game I could. The World Series back in '86, now that was something. I had this old Zenith television set, the kind with the wooden cabinet, you know? Weighed an absolute ton. Took two grown men just to move it across the living room. The picture tube finally went out on it in the middle of a thunderstorm. Lightning struck the transformer down the street—sounded like a cannon going off. Knocked the power out for three days. \n\nWe had to eat everything out of the freezer before it spoiled. My wife made this huge pot of chili with all the frozen ground beef. We ate chili for breakfast, lunch, and dinner. She used to put a little bit of cinnamon in her chili, just a pinch. Said it gave it a deeper flavor. Her grandmother taught her that. She came over from Italy on a boat when she was just a girl. Ellis Island and everything. They had to change their last name because the man at the desk couldn't spell it right...\n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Stopped caring? I don't know. I just sit in the chair mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer care about things like baseball or church, or is it more that you still care but can’t seem to make yourself do them anymore?\n\nPatient: Care? Well, I suppose I still care. It's not like I stopped liking them or anything. I just... I can't seem to get up and go do them. \n\nLike the church, you know. I still think about going. I even ironed my good trousers last Saturday night. Put a sharp crease right down the front. You have to use a damp cloth when you iron them, otherwise the fabric gets shiny. My wife used to have this heavy iron, a real old-fashioned one. You had to fill it with water from a little plastic cup, and it would hiss and spit steam everywhere. She burned her wrist on it once, right before my niece's wedding. We had to stop at the pharmacy for ointment on the way to the reception. \n\nThe reception was at this big hall downtown. They served chicken cordon bleu. It was a bit dry, if I'm being honest. But the band was good. They played all the old standards. Frank Sinatra, Dean Martin. I used to have a whole collection of their records on vinyl. Kept them in milk crates in the basement. The basement flooded back in 2005, ruined the whole lot of them. The water heater burst right in the middle of the night. Sounded like a waterfall down there. I had to call the plumber at three in the morning...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Making myself do things. Yeah, I just can't seem to get out of the chair these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or that you're failing the people who care about you, like your family or your church community?\n\nPatient: Failing people? Not worth much? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel like a failure or anything. I've always been a hard worker, earned my keep, you know? \n\nI worked stocking shelves and filling orders down at the grocery store for years. Best they had in the canned goods section. You have to rotate the stock, see—older cans in the front, newer in the back. A lot of the young kids they hire today just don't understand that. They just shove the new cans right on the front of the shelf. Like this one kid we had, Kevin. Nice enough boy, but he dropped a whole case of diced tomatoes once in the back storage room. What an absolute mess. Red juice everywhere, looked like a crime scene. We had to get the big industrial mop out. \n\nThat mop bucket had a squeaky wheel. Drove the floor manager, Mr. Henderson, completely up the wall. Squeak, squeak, squeak all the way down aisle four. Henderson was a stickler for noise. He used to wear these thick rubber-soled shoes so you couldn't hear him sneaking up behind you. Hush Puppies, I think they were called. My brother had a pair of those in brown suede. Wore them to a dance once and stepped in a deep mud puddle... ruined them completely. He tried to dry them by the radiator in the hallway and they shrunk up like curled leaves. My mother was so mad she made him pay for them out of his paper route money.\n\nAnyway... what did you ask me? About being worth something? No, I don't feel worthless. I just... haven't been doing much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I don't think so. I'm not restless or anything. I'm just sitting in my chair mostly. \n\nI mean, I'm not moving as fast as I used to, but I'm 74, you know? I used to be pretty quick on my feet. And behind the wheel, too. I had this 1968 Ford Torino. Now that car could move. Powder blue, with those vinyl seats that would absolutely burn the back of your legs in the summertime if you wore shorts. You had to roll the windows down for five minutes just to let the heat out before you could even sit. Hand-crank windows, none of this power button nonsense they put in cars today. The handle on the passenger side kept falling off, actually. I had to keep it in the glovebox. \n\nThe glovebox didn't latch right either, ever since my son slammed it too hard trying to kill a wasp that got in the car. Huge wasp, terrifying thing. We were on our way to the state fair when it happened. They had this giant pumpkin contest at the fair every year. I remember seeing one that weighed over eight hundred pounds. Took a whole forklift just to get it onto the scale. The farmer who grew it wore these bright striped overalls. Said his secret was using fish emulsion in the soil. Smelled awful, I imagine, but you can't argue with an eight-hundred-pound pumpkin...\n\nI'm sorry, what was the question? Something about sitting still? No, I'm not restless. Like I said, I just don't do much moving at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless inside, even if you're not showing it or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Down? No, I wouldn't use those words. I'm not sitting here crying or feeling sorry for myself or anything like that. I just... well, I mostly just sit in my chair. \n\nIt's a very good chair, to be fair. A genuine La-Z-Boy. I bought it at Sears back in... oh, must have been 1992. Back when they still had those massive catalogs, you remember those? You could order just about anything out of them. My mother actually ordered a whole sewing machine from one when I was a boy. A Singer. Heavy metal thing, painted black with gold trim, weighed an absolute ton. The mailman, Gary, nearly threw his back out carrying it up our front steps. Gary was a good fella. He wore those little blue uniform shorts even when it got cold in November. He had this beagle named Buster that he’d sometimes let ride in the mail truck. Buster had a terrible habit of chasing the neighborhood squirrels. One time, he chased a big fat gray one right up the oak tree in Mrs. Gable's yard, and the squirrel jumped onto her roof and got straight into her attic through a loose shingle. She had to call pest control, and they set up all these little wire traps baited with peanut butter... \n\nI'm sorry, my mind is wandering again. What did you ask me? About feeling down? No, I don't feel hopeless inside. Like I said, I just don't have the drive to get up and do much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even the things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy things just fine, I haven't lost interest. I just don't do them as much. \n\nLike baseball, I still like baseball. I used to listen to the games on this old Zenith transistor radio I had. Silver front, came in a little black leather case with a carrying strap. That strap eventually snapped, though. I tried to fix it with some duct tape, but the tape got all gummy in the summer heat. You know how duct tape gets when it's left in the sun? Leaves that awful sticky gray residue on absolutely everything it touches. I got it all over my good slacks once. Khaki pants I bought for my nephew's high school graduation. \n\nHe went on to the state university to get a degree in... oh, what was it? Geology, I think. Rocks. He loved rocks. Used to carry a motorized rock tumbler right in the trunk of his car. Loudest thing you ever heard, just grinding and tumbling away back there while he drove. He drove a Honda Civic, one of those early ones from the seventies that looked like a little roller skate. It was bright yellow. You really don't see many yellow cars anymore, do you? Used to be you'd see yellow cars, mint green cars, two-tone cars with white roofs. Now everything on the highway is just silver or black or gray. It's very boring to look at when you're sitting out on the porch watching traffic go by. My neighbor, Mr. Wallace, he just bought a silver SUV. Barely fits in his driveway. He had to cut back his prize-winning hydrangea bushes just to open the passenger door...\n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Losing interest in things? No, no, I haven't lost interest. I still like what I like. I just... well, like I said, I mostly just sit in my chair these days."
  }
